A ruling that treats institutional resource allocation as following layer assignment regardless of cultural effect preserves the current doctrine and exposes the civilization to continued coalition expansion. A ruling that allows aggregate regulatory effect to trigger fiscal recalibration preserves the proportional-benefit principle and introduces a new constitutional category, the culturally self-sorted sub-layer, into doctrine. Either ruling will have multi-century consequences, and the coalition problem is where those consequences will first compound.

Main is where this problem happens because it is the layer with enough population, enough cultural variety and enough Article XXVIII petition capacity for coalitions to form at scale. The problem is structurally Main’s and could not arise elsewhere. A Sanctuary coalition culturally replicating a lower-layer environment would face the behavioral threshold and phase out of Sanctuary before the coalition could stabilize. A lower-layer coalition culturally replicating Main could not access Main’s institutional services regardless of its behavior, because its residents do not reside in Main. Only Main’s scale, variety and institutional investment together make cultural self-sorting at coalition scale a doctrinal question rather than a theoretical one.

The Weight of the Proving Ground

Sanctuary is optimized. The lower layers each have a settled character. Main is the only layer where the question of how to live is genuinely open, and that openness is both the layer’s most distinctive feature and its most demanding condition.

A Sanctuary resident knows what Sanctuary is for. A -3 resident knows what -3 is for. A -1 or -2 resident, whatever the route of arrival, knows their environment’s character within weeks of arrival. The conditions themselves settle the question of what to do with them. Sanctuary residents pursue the curated life, and lower-layer residents pursue the specific forms of life their layer’s institutional character makes available. The environment does much of the structuring that an individual in an Earth-era context would have had to do themselves.

Main residents do not have this structuring. The layer contains every life pattern simultaneously: ascending, descending, stable, wandering, traveling, settling, curating toward Sanctuary, drifting toward -1, building toward whatever SAD-equivalent credential set the resident can stack within Main’s constraints. Every choice is available and no choice is obvious. Whether to pursue ascension, business, family or travel, or whether to settle: all of these options are open, all produce different lives, and the layer does not recommend any of them. The Charter sets the rules and leaves the purpose of each life to the resident.

This is what the proving ground designation captures when it operates at its most honest. The system has no specific test it is running on residents. Main is a proving ground because its conditions require each resident to generate the structure themselves: to decide, across decades and centuries, what a life made of these options should look like. Residents who choose well produce distinctive lives at scale. Residents who choose poorly or choose nothing drift across decades without the environment producing the structure on their behalf. Both outcomes are possible within the architecture, and the architecture does not prevent either.

The cost of Main’s freedom is this burden of self-structuring across long lives. The benefit is that the residents who generate structure well produce the civilization’s most original work, its most distinctive businesses, its most complex cultural output and its most texturally rich relationships. Main’s proving ground function is real in both directions: it rewards the residents who rise to its conditions and does not protect the residents who do not. A Main resident at ninety STI who produces a century of sustained work and a Main resident at forty STI who never consolidated a trajectory differ in how each answered the question the layer’s openness placed in front of them. The difference is not one of moral character. Both answers are legitimate, and the layer accommodates both.

The Student’s Error

The student who reads this resource and concludes that Main is the civilization’s default layer rather than its metabolic center has misread the architecture. Default implies a starting position residents move past. Metabolic center implies the organ through which the civilization’s ongoing function passes, and Main is a metabolic center, not a default. For the majority of residents most of the time, their real lives are in Main. Sanctuary is a destination some residents reach, some pass through and some visit without settling. The punitive layers are destinations residents are sorted into by their conduct. Main is the ongoing condition from which every layer is defined and to which every layer relates.

A common misreading holds that Main’s full STI spectrum is the layer’s defining problem. In fact the spectrum is the layer’s contribution to the civilization and the specific condition that makes Main Main. It produces the cultural and civilizational outputs no narrower-spectrum layer could generate. A civilization with only Sanctuary and the punitive layers would have no Main, and so no three-billion-person layer where the full range of conduct coexists and interacts. Every cultural product that emerges from that coexistence would be absent.

Reading the coalition problem as an immediate crisis mistakes the timeline. The coalition problem is a structural mechanism that operates continuously at every scale, and its consequences compound across centuries. At any given moment, most Main districts are not operating as coalitions, most coalitions that do form are too small for their asymmetric institutional consumption to be architecturally significant, and most that reach significant scale produce regulatory environments that are culturally distinctive without replicating a lower layer’s full character. The problem reaches architectural weight only when a coalition reaches enough scale and enough cultural coherence to make the asymmetry unignorable, and the civilization may go decades or centuries between such encounters. The problem is real but slow. The architecture’s response will come when the first sufficiently large coalition surfaces, not before.

Main is the layer the rest of the architecture is built around: the civilization’s body, with the other layers as organs specialized for functions the body needs but cannot perform at scale. Sanctuary holds the high-trust reservoir. The punitive layers hold the consequence gradient. Main is where the civilization lives.

Resource 27: Life in -3 Terminal

A Sociological Studies Textbook Excerpt — The Permanent Boundary, Private Justice as Ecology, and What the Federal Floor Actually Leaves

-3 Terminal has the strongest architectural identity of any layer and the weakest institutional footprint. Death is final. The federal floor provides UBI, Article XXV enforcement, and facilitated child relocation, and nothing beyond. Everything else, from commerce and justice to community and daily safety, comes from the residents themselves or does not exist at all. This resource covers the sociology the layer’s architectural description leaves open: how private justice prices and selects, how the punitive and voluntary populations stabilize their coexistence over time, what the voluntary districts produce at scale, what the colosseum zone permits that no other layer does, and what the layer shows about the civilization as the place where most of it has been withdrawn.

The Permanent Boundary

Every other layer in VMSS assumes continuity. A resident who dies in Sanctuary, Main, -1, or -2 is revived through backup vessel infrastructure. The revival rate varies by layer, but the commitment is the same: death is reversible in the overwhelming majority of cases, and the civilization’s continuity doctrine applies. -3 breaks this. The implant severs the backup vessel link programmatically at the moment of terminal reassignment. The severance is hardware-level rather than policy-level, and no override or emergency mechanism restores it. From the moment a resident crosses the -3 boundary, death is terminal.

This is the layer’s defining feature, and it shapes every other one. The voluntary population knew it when they chose -3. The punitive population crossed a threshold where the civilization’s commitment to continuity no longer applied. Either way, a -3 resident’s relationship to mortality is categorically different from that of anyone above the terminal boundary, and every decision they make operates under a constraint the other layers have removed. Backup vessel infrastructure does not undo physical harm sustained in -3. Its consequences persist through whatever reconstructive medicine the layer’s private infrastructure can provide, which is less than Main offers as standard.

The permanence expresses the layer’s place in the consequence gradient; terror is not its purpose. The upper four layers commit to preserving their residents’ lives because the conduct that placed them there warrants the commitment. -3 residents either crossed the threshold where the commitment no longer applies or chose an environment without it, and the layer’s design treats this as the defining fact. The thin institutional presence, the private justice apparatus, the colosseum classification and the unregulated economy all follow from the permanent boundary. Without the boundary, -3 would be a more libertarian -2, which is a different layer entirely.

The Two-Population Sort

-3’s population is a mixture. The ratio is not canonically fixed, and the civilization deliberately leaves it open to inference, but the two populations are structurally distinct and their coexistence has measurable patterns.

The punitive cohort arrived through layer reassignment, after conduct that crossed the layer’s qualifying thresholds: murder, serious predation, or accumulated conduct that exceeded -2’s bounds. The permanent boundary was applied to them; they did not choose it. Their relationship to the layer is primarily that of residents living under a condition they were placed into, rather than of inhabitants making a home. Some stabilize, build lives, and adapt to the layer’s character. Others deteriorate or never stabilize. The layer does not intervene either way.

The voluntary cohort arrived by choice, through the voluntary permanent residency framework or by direct filing after arriving from Earth. Two conditions filter that choice: a libertarian disposition strong enough to find upper-layer institutional presence intolerable rather than merely inconvenient, and a relationship to mortality settled enough that the permanent boundary registered as a cost worth paying rather than a deal-breaker. Residents who reach -3 voluntarily have passed both filters. Those screened out by either one remain in -2 or above.

The two cohorts sort spatially without the civilization arranging it. The voluntary population concentrates in built-out districts: market associations operating under reputation ledgers, private infrastructure maintained through cooperative dues, and settlements with organized internal norms that voluntary residents built or deliberately joined. The punitive population concentrates in areas voluntary residents have not claimed: the frontier peripheries, contested zones without stable cooperative governance, and neighborhoods where the layer’s institutional withdrawal meets no organized private replacement. Nothing in the architecture enforces the sort. Voluntary residents have the resources, organization and motivation to build districts that match their preferences, and punitive residents who lack those resources or that organization land in whatever terrain the voluntary districts have not yet absorbed.

The coexistence is stable at scale for one specific reason: the voluntary districts have no incentive to expand into the contested zones, and the contested zones have no coherent organization to resist containment. The voluntary cohort’s reputation-ledger economy produces commerce and social order within its districts, and residents can move between voluntary districts without disruption. The punitive cohort stays where it is, subject to whatever private enforcement the local terrain produces. It has limited access to the voluntary commerce network and limited ability to join voluntary districts without meeting the reputation-ledger admission thresholds those districts enforce. Two parallel civilizations occupy the same geography, and that parallel arrangement is the sorting mechanism that lets them coexist.

The Voluntary Cohort and Its Filters

The voluntary libertarian resident is the layer’s most analytically significant population. They are not the majority numerically, but they built most of the infrastructure and produce most of the observable daily life. Their selection filters are worth analyzing because the filters determine what the layer actually is.

The first filter is the libertarian disposition. Residents above -3 who find institutional presence merely inconvenient have other options: lower taxation in -1, speculative market access in -1 that upper layers exclude, and private economic operation in -2 without the reputation-ledger gates -3 imposes. For them, -1 or -2 suffice. Residents who choose -3 specifically object to institutional presence itself, not merely to its cost. They want minimal governance, meaning the federal floor only, rather than lower taxation, reduced regulation or lighter enforcement. Their disposition is libertarianism taken further than Earth libertarians typically wanted to go, because Earth-era conditions offered no layer where the disposition could be lived at its full commitment.

The second filter is a settled relationship to mortality. A resident who finds the permanent boundary intolerable does not descend to -3, whatever their politics; backup vessel coverage matters enough to them that losing it is disqualifying. A resident who descends voluntarily has worked through this, whether through philosophical settlement, experience with environments that made mortality familiar, or specific motivations where the layer’s offerings outweigh the loss of revival infrastructure. The filter is real: most Earth-era libertarians, transplanted into VMSS, would not choose -3 if they understood what they were giving up. The voluntary residents are the subset for whom both filters align.

Together the two filters produce a distinctive population. Voluntary -3 residents tend to be unusually self-directed, since the decision to descend is itself evidence of agency working against institutional defaults. They tend to be practically competent, because without institutional infrastructure, residents who cannot solve problems privately cannot thrive there. They tend to have either substantial capital, which the layer’s low taxation and unregulated commerce reward efficiently, or substantial skills, which its reputation-ledger market associations can absorb profitably. The result is a small population that resembles neither the layer’s punitive cohort nor the population of any upper layer, a demographic that exists nowhere else in the civilization.

The layer’s canonical voluntary-resident archetype is Elias Varro, a Main Layer operator who filed for voluntary permanent residency at thirty-four after eighteen months of research into the layer’s actual conditions. His arrival strategy was patience: a first year building relationships rather than revenue, every obligation paid early, and a gradual reputation as someone whose word held. By year two he had identified the gap no institutional -3 backstop could fill (no reliable private financial infrastructure, no lending or capital allocation beyond personal relationships) and had begun a private lending operation that became the most significant source of private capital in two districts by year four. His defining moral test arrived in year seven, when a significant borrower defaulted and left the district. Varro absorbed the loss without aggressive pursuit, documented the default clearly, and made the documentation available to anyone who asked. Within three months two new clients approached him specifically because of how he handled it. They had been watching to see whether he would respond with intimidation economics. By his fifth decade he operated across four districts with twenty-eight employees, most on twenty-hour qualifying schedules that collected -3’s Primary Job Subsidy on top of wages. The layer’s voluntary-cohort literature sums up his life in a line it frequently cites: “He chose this. It turned out to be the right choice. Those two facts together are sufficient.”

Private Justice as Ecology

The layer’s private justice apparatus is widely documented at the mechanical level. What the mechanical description does not answer is how the apparatus prices, selects, and fails.

Private justice on -3 combines reputation ledgers, market associations, private security cooperatives, and customary-law formations specific to particular enterprises or districts. The reputation ledger carries the most weight. Every voluntary-district resident has a ledger that tracks contract fulfillment, dispute history, vouching relationships, and behavioral patterns visible to the market. The ledger is portable across district lines within the voluntary commerce network, so a resident who defaults on a contract in one district finds the default visible in every other district their ledger reaches. A reliable record produces access, opportunity and market position that a resident without one cannot reach.

Pricing runs through the ledger. A contract’s terms are set partly by the goods or services exchanged and partly by the parties’ ledger positions. A resident with a strong record pays less for private services, gets better contract terms, and faces fewer deposit or collateral requirements. A resident with a weak record pays premiums that reflect the counterparty’s risk of default. A resident with no record at all, such as a new arrival from either cohort, faces refused transactions or heavily collateralized terms until they build a record the network can read. The market prices the ledger as much as it prices the underlying exchange.

Selection runs through market-association admission. A voluntary district’s market association admits residents whose ledger meets the association’s threshold. Residents below the threshold can operate in the district but cannot access the network’s contract infrastructure, dispute resolution mechanisms, or preferred pricing. This selection gives each district its character: higher-threshold associations concentrate higher-ledger residents, produce lower contract friction, and sustain more sophisticated commerce. Lower-threshold associations admit broader populations and operate with higher friction. The tiering is continuous across districts, and residents sort themselves into the associations whose thresholds their ledger can meet.

The apparatus fails in specific ways. Reputation ledgers are only as reliable as the signal inputs the network uses, and deliberate corruption of inputs is a known problem: false vouching, collusive positive ratings, coordinated negative ratings against competitors. The network responds reputationally, since discovered corruption degrades the corruptors’ ledgers more severely than the corruption benefited them, but the response operates after the fact rather than preventively. Private security has the same limit: a cooperative that fails to deliver on its security commitments faces ledger consequences, but only after the failure has cost residents. The apparatus does not substitute for institutional justice. It produces different outcomes, including failures the institutional apparatus would not have produced and successes it would not have achieved.

The Colosseum Zone

The upper layers do not permit certain categories of enterprise: paying-audience predator encounters with expected human fatalities; gladiatorial combat between consenting adults to terminal outcome; full-access extreme-risk environments where the risk is real and the mitigation is not the institution’s responsibility; and post-apocalyptic aesthetic spaces where the environment’s legal and physical conditions are part of the product. Some residents want to build these enterprises and others want to use them. The upper layers refuse them for reasons the Charter treats as non-negotiable: the harm threshold, the backup vessel commitment, and the institutional character that governs the layer’s ambient conditions.

-3 permits them through a customary-law mechanism the doctrinal literature names the colosseum classification. The mechanism is the gate-contract: an enterprise meets the colosseum definition the moment its gate enforces open declaration of risk, informed entry, and the absence of deception about what waits inside the perimeter. The visitor passes through the gate at their own risk. Death is declared openly as a possible outcome alongside survival, and the visitor knows it before the gate closes behind them. A death inside is what the visitor paid to come near. The operator is not liable, because the layer does not count such a death as an injury: stepping through the gate signs a contract, and the death fulfills it.

The classification attaches automatically, with no permit, application or institutional sanction. -3’s private justice infrastructure does not investigate colosseum fatalities, for the same reason it does not investigate the outcome of a duel both parties agreed to. There is no inquest, detention, review board or cooperative-level scrutiny of the operator’s conduct, because that conduct is what the contract authorised. The operator is exempt from after-the-fact review as long as the gate-contract was honestly constructed and the visitor was not deceived about what they were entering.

The Saurian Park is the canonical operating precedent. An industrialist who had descended voluntarily built what the upper layers had refused to permit: unmanaged predator encounters with expected fatalities, with participants paying to enter an environment whose predators would kill some of them at the actuarial rate the original specifications projected. The park operates continuously because the gate-contract was honestly constructed, the visitors are not deceived, and the colosseum classification attached on the day the first paying visitor walked through. Other enterprises operate under the same mechanism: live re-enactment environments where conscious participants engage in scripted-but-real frontier scenarios with real drugs, real sex, real violence, and real death as part of the contracted experience; duels between consenting adults to terminal outcome (illegal above -3 because consent cannot override the prohibition on killing, legal in -3 through the same gate-contract mechanism); extreme-risk stunt operations financed by operators willing to absorb the loss and participants willing to sign the gate-contract; drug markets operating without upper-layer regulatory frameworks; adult industries operating without upper-layer protective frameworks.

The layer’s doctrinal literature calls this ecology the wild west, in the exact sense of the phrase. The wild west had law: whatever the local sheriff was willing to enforce, and the sheriff was one person with his own loyalties, his own price, and his own sense of what mattered. -3 is that model scaled up and distributed across hundreds of districts, each with its own cooperative, contractor or community structure and its own threshold for what it will tolerate and what it will charge to look away. The colosseum classification settles how a death inside the gate is treated; whether an enterprise can operate at all is a separate question. More broadly, an enterprise can operate in -3 the moment the local apparatus decides not to interfere with it. There is no license or permit, only the question of whether the people who would have stopped you have decided not to. That question is answered five different ways: by indifference, by payment, by mutual benefit, by reputational alignment, or by formal endorsement. The result is a different regulatory regime rather than anarchy, with rules written, enforced and priced by local actors.

In practice the zone is narrower than the upper-layer imagination typically assumes. The voluntary districts produce a small number of colosseum enterprises, concentrated in specific operators’ territories and serving specific clientele who travel to them deliberately. The rest of -3’s voluntary life runs on commerce, settlement and cooperative infrastructure that resembles frontier environments across history more than exotic libertarian fantasies. The colosseum zone is the layer’s distinctive feature at its edges, not its modal condition. Most voluntary residents do not operate colosseum enterprises, do not participate in them, and do not want to; they chose -3 for the minimal governance and the permanent boundary. The gladiators exist because -3 is the only place they can, and the rest of the layer goes about its everyday life around them.

The Federal Floor in Practice

The federal floor on -3 stops at a specific minimum. The analytical question is what the minimum actually produces at the resident level, because the floor is the only institutional contact most -3 residents have with the civilization.

UBI distribution operates continuously. Every -3 resident receives the layer’s baseline currency transfer on the standard schedule, credited through the implant system for residents who retain implants and through private bank accounts for those who have removed them. The amount is the layer’s fraction of the upper-layer baseline, calibrated to the layer’s purchasing-power gradient. Delivery is automated and cannot be interrupted by local events: a district collapsing into contested conflict still receives its UBI, because the federal infrastructure operates outside local conditions. This is the civilization’s minimum commitment to its -3 residents: no one will be allowed to starve through federal abandonment, whatever their local conditions produce.

Article XXV federal law enforcement operates against specific categories: nuclear weapons, implant hacking at institutional scale, and organized sovereignty threats to the civilization itself. These are civilizational-level violations, and -3’s local conditions do not limit the federal response. It runs through national-defense-tier infrastructure (surveillance, identification, and targeted action) that does not require ground-level institutional presence to function. A resident building a nuclear device in -3 receives the same federal response as a resident attempting it in Sanctuary.

Child relocation is federally facilitated. Any child born in -3 retains the standing right to relocate to Main Layer at any age, and the federal apparatus delivers the relocation when the child exercises the right. The mechanism does not require parental consent and cannot be blocked by local conditions. A child who files the request receives federal transport to a Main Layer autoparenting facility within a window the system is designed to meet wherever in -3 the child is located. This is the floor’s most visible commitment to a population the layer’s design treats as categorically distinct: residents who did not reach -3 through conduct or voluntary filing retain full access to the architecture above.

Everything else is absent: no drone patrols, no AI governance enforcement against daily conduct, no institutional medical infrastructure, no federal dispute resolution, no institutional commerce regulation. The floor is a minimum rather than a system of governance. Residents live within whatever conditions their local districts produce, and the federal contact is limited to UBI arrival, Article XXV monitoring for civilizational threats, and child-relocation fulfillment when requested. A resident who asks what VMSS does for them on -3 gets a narrow answer: the civilization delivers their UBI and will respond if they threaten it at civilizational scale. Everything else is whatever they and their neighbors build, or whatever the local terrain produces when nothing is built.

One federal provision specifically governs the interface between -3 and the upper layers: vessel-link parity (LP-004.2, Backup Vessel Parity, a federal law enacted under Article XXV.VI). Without it, a Main Layer visitor with active backup vessel coverage could, in principle, take on -3’s highest-mortality work (colosseum operations, private security, dangerous extraction labor) at zero mortality risk to themselves. They would die, revive, and collect the premium wages -3 pays for work that native residents correctly price as mortality-dependent. The economic asymmetry would be catastrophic: -3’s most dangerous and most lucrative work would flow to visitors whose continuity the layer does not offer its own residents, and native residents would be priced out of work on their own layer. LP-006 separately mandates disclosure of backup vessel status in all economic transactions, employment contracts, and enterprise participation within -3. Under LP-004.2, backup vessel coverage also does not cross the -3 boundary: entry requires documented vessel-link suspension, the link stays severed for the duration of the visit, and it is restored on exit. Death inside -3 is final for visitor and resident alike, so the mortality gap that would have made the visit exploitative never opens. A Main Layer visitor can operate a capital allocation firm in -3, trade in non-mortality markets, run a lending business, or provide services, and takes any mortality-risk work on the same terminal terms as the residents who price it. The federal reach closes the exploit without expanding federal governance of the layer’s daily life.

The Student’s Error

The student who reads this resource and concludes that -3 is VMSS’s failure layer has misread the architecture. The layer deliberately expresses what the civilization is willing to commit to at the terminal edge of its consequence gradient. The federal floor is a choice: residents whose conduct reached the layer’s thresholds, and residents who chose the layer’s conditions voluntarily, receive the architectural support the layer’s position in the gradient warrants and no more. A higher floor would violate the gradient’s proportionality. A lower floor would fail the commitment to human life the founding core preserves. The civilization set the floor where it honours both constraints.

It is tempting to read -3 as anarchist or stateless; the layer in fact has an institutional floor: UBI arrives, Article XXV applies, and children have standing rights. The civilization delivers these state commitments continuously. They seem invisible only because residents accustomed to upper-layer institutional densities register them as absences rather than as a deliberate floor. A genuinely stateless layer would have no UBI, no civilizational-threat response, and no child relocation framework. -3 has all three. What it lacks is daily governance, and a layer without daily governance still has governance: the specific kind the civilization chose to provide where the consequence gradient’s terminal edge sits.

Another misreading treats the voluntary cohort as a small anomaly within a predominantly punitive population, which misses its architectural importance. Numerically the voluntary residents may be a minority at any given moment (the civilization leaves this open), but they produce most of the observable civilization the layer sustains. They built the voluntary districts, the market associations, the reputation-ledger commerce, the cooperative infrastructure, and the colosseum-classified enterprises that set -3 apart from a more libertarian -2. The punitive cohort inhabits whatever terrain the voluntary cohort has not claimed. A reading that treats the layer as primarily punitive with a voluntary fringe has it backward: the layer is primarily what its voluntary residents build, with a punitive population living in the spaces their infrastructure does not reach.

Finally, -3 is defined by what VMSS withdraws rather than by what it provides. Every other layer is defined in part by what VMSS provides: pre-intervention enforcement, behavioral sorting, institutional density, post-intervention response, backup vessel continuity, regulatory infrastructure. On -3, what stays is a specific kind of presence: the federal floor, the civilizational-threat response, and the child relocation right. The layer is designed to answer what remains after the institution has pulled back. The answer is the residents themselves, the voluntary infrastructure they build, the reputation economy they sustain, the colosseum zone they construct at their edges, and the federal floor the civilization keeps delivering regardless of their local conditions. The upper layers add institutional architecture on top of this foundation; -3 is the foundation without the additions. The name Freedom Layer is meant literally. It describes freedom operating at its full weight with only the federal floor underneath, and what the residents build on that floor is the layer’s actual character.

Resource 28: Backup Vessel Architecture

A Technical and Philosophical Studies Textbook Excerpt — Continuity as Doctrine, the Captured-Cognition Problem, and the Questions the Architecture Leaves Open

Backup vessel infrastructure is the civilization’s continuity mechanism. Every Charter provision involving revival, every layer’s institutional character and every enforcement posture’s proportionality depends on the architecture working reliably enough to support the doctrinal commitments built on it. The Charter establishes the mechanism, the whitepaper specifies the five failure modes, and the Academy’s Q22 A+ response identifies the captured-cognition problem the mechanism surfaces but does not resolve. This resource extends those foundations. The civilization’s official position is that revival preserves continuity, but the architecture that produces that continuity raises questions the doctrine has not fully closed, and this resource takes them up.

Continuity as Doctrine, Not Metaphysics

Whether a revived citizen is “the same person” admits competing answers under every framework that examines it seriously. Teleporter paradoxes, Parfit-style identity puzzles, replica-and-original thought experiments: the tradition is long and the arguments do not converge. The civilization does not try to resolve the philosophical question. It makes the question operationally irrelevant.

Continuity-not-innocence is a doctrinal commitment and makes no metaphysical claim. The civilization takes no position on whether revival preserves the numerically same consciousness or produces a consciousness continuous enough with the original that the distinction fails to matter. It takes a position only on how it will treat the revived person. The ledger, the STI score, the layer assignment, the SAD stack and the criminal record all persist. Every institutional fact that identified the resident before death identifies them after revival, with no interruption, no reclassification, and no procedural step that would register revival as a discontinuity. A Sanctuary resident who dies on Tuesday and revives on Wednesday returns to their apartment still a Sanctuary resident, still holding their RIL and CCD memberships, still owing on their contracts and still carrying their conduct record. Because the civilization treats them as the same person, they are the same person for every purpose the civilization adjudicates.

The approach deliberately separates the philosophical question from the operational framework, and it involves no metaphysical sleight of hand. The framework produces every effect that would follow if the metaphysical claim held true, without having to defend that claim on its own terms. A civilization committed to the metaphysical claim would have to defend it against every philosophical tradition that challenges it. One committed only to the operational treatment can accommodate, within the same doctrinal framework, residents who believe revival preserves consciousness, residents who believe it produces a continuous copy, and residents who think the question has no determinate answer. Each resident’s personal view of what revival means to them is private cognition the civilization does not touch. The civilization’s position concerns what it will do rather than what is true, and that distinction lets continuity function without consensus on the underlying question.

The Sync Architecture

Mind-state synchronization is periodic rather than continuous. This is doctrinal: each sync is a consented event (LP-038.2), and a resident’s backup vessel holds the mind-state captured at their last sync.

Under periodic sync, the Academy’s Q22 scenario, in which Dr. Yara Osei dies in a structural collapse and wakes in a backup vessel with the last 47 minutes of her experience missing, is the architecture’s ordinary output: her last sync ran 47 minutes before she died.

Each sync captures the full neural state, which is broader than the resident’s conscious experience. Unsent messages, unexpressed thoughts, physiological stress responses and draft communications the resident composed and decided not to send all exist within the captured mind-state, because the capture does not distinguish thought the resident chose to act on from thought the resident did not. The sync captures the substrate, and the substrate holds both, since at the neural level thought consists of patterns the resident may or may not choose to consolidate into action.

Q22’s A+ response calls this territory the captured-cognition problem. The Charter’s privacy architecture commits to cognition being non-public: thought produces no consequence, only outward expression does. Sync captures cognition as part of its ordinary operation. The capture is not an attempt to police thought. Sync that separated thought-to-be-expressed from thought-to-be-private would need a prior judgment about which category a given neural state belongs in, and the capture layer cannot make that judgment without knowing the thought’s subsequent trajectory. The architecture captures everything and leaves classification to the layers above the capture. The tension between capture and privacy is structural, and the civilization has not fully resolved where the line between them should fall.

The Five Failure Modes

Whitepaper §17.1.2 enumerates five failure modes beyond terminal layer severance, and notes that these push effective death rates 10 to 50 times higher than the published revival failure rates would suggest in isolation. The enumeration is canonical. This section asks how the modes interact and what the 10–50x multiplier means for residents’ actual mortality exposure.

Implant removal is the most straightforward failure. A resident who voluntarily removes their implant severs the backup vessel link. The vessel may continue to exist in whatever state the resident’s last complete sync produced, but no further synchronization occurs, and revival eligibility depends on the pre-removal state rather than the resident’s current condition. Voluntary implant removal is rare in the upper layers, where the implant is a condition of residency and removing it means leaving, and more common in lower layers, where removal carries fewer structural consequences. A resident who removes their implant in -1 or -2 and later dies faces a revival process operating from whatever pre-removal state the vessel holds, with all intervening experience unrecoverable.

Economic inability captures the cases where a resident cannot afford the backup vessel’s ongoing maintenance costs. Vessels require biological substrate, fabrication resources, and continuous infrastructure allocation. The upper layers absorb these costs through institutional infrastructure and lower layers deliver them through fabrication proxy installations. In marginal cases, though, the maintenance may not be continuous: residents in deeply economically distressed situations, residents whose local cooperative infrastructure has failed, or residents whose commitments to private providers lapsed. A vessel whose maintenance has lapsed is not guaranteed to be revival-ready when needed.

Fabrication glitches are defects that emerge during revival itself. The vessel is fabricated from feedstock and assembled at the proxy installation, and the fabrication process operates at tolerances where occasional undetected defects compound. The defect may be biological, such as organ systems that fail under the stress of transfer initiation or neural architectures with subtle imperfections the pre-transfer inspection does not catch. Or it may be procedural, such as transfer timing off by microseconds in ways that affect integration. Most fabrication glitches manifest during revival and produce the binary failure the doctrine documents: the resident does not wake up, or wakes up and deteriorates beyond the system’s recovery capacity.

Supply chain shortages affect the fabrication proxy’s capacity to deliver revivals on schedule. Fabrication technology does not make a civilization immune to distributional failures in feedstock availability, energy allocation, or fabrication facility throughput at peak demand. A revival queue that backs up during a mass casualty event has consequences for residents: revivals delayed past viability windows, vessels held in stasis longer than the architecture was designed to support, and facilities running at capacities their engineering margins do not accommodate.

Biological rejection is the irreducible floor. Some percentage of mind-state transfers fail at the neural integration step regardless of the fabrication’s quality, the sync’s completeness, or the infrastructure’s availability. The vessel’s architecture rejects the incoming mind-state the way an immune system rejects a transplanted organ, through a fundamental incompatibility between the new architecture and the patterns being integrated into it rather than through any correctable error. Biological rejection rates are low but not zero, and no technological improvement the civilization has deployed has reduced them to zero across centuries of operation.

The 10–50x multiplier emerges from the compound operation of these modes. A resident whose revival failure rate would be 1 in 1,000,000 under pure biological rejection may face an effective death rate of 1 in 20,000 when all five modes are factored in across a lifetime of exposure. The multiplier is not uniform across the population. Residents in upper layers with continuous implant coverage, economic stability, and access to the highest-maintenance infrastructure experience death rates much closer to the biological floor. Residents whose situations stress several failure modes at once (intermittent implant coverage, economic distress, lower-layer infrastructure) experience rates at the higher end of the multiplier. The civilization publishes the biological floor as the revival failure rate because that is the rate the architecture itself produces. Residents’ actual exposure is meaningfully higher, and honest publication also acknowledges the multiplier.

Visitor Revival and the Origin-Layer Rule

A visitor’s backup vessel synchronizes to the fabrication infrastructure of their origin layer rather than the destination layer. This is doctrinal (Whitepaper §17.1.1), and it has consequences. A Main Layer citizen visiting -2 retains the Main Layer revival rate of approximately 1 in 1,000,000 because their backup vessel is fabricated at Main Layer facilities, not at the -2 fabrication proxy installation. If they die on -2, Main Layer infrastructure revives them; they wake up in their Main Layer vessel and arrive back in Main with continuity preserved. The origin-layer rule is what makes visiting a layer a different experience from residing in it: visitors carry their origin-layer revival architecture into the lower-layer environments they enter, with the -3 exception described below.

The rule switches at reassignment or voluntary permanent residency. Punitive reassignment to -1 or below severs the origin-layer link at the hardware level and automatically binds the citizen to the destination layer’s fabrication proxy installation at the corresponding failure rate. Voluntary permanent residency does the same by election. The citizen’s vessel is relocated, rebuilt, or reinitialized at the destination layer’s infrastructure, and later revivals occur under the destination’s conditions rather than the origin’s. Elective residency, which preserves origin-layer status, does not trigger the switch: the elective resident retains origin-layer revival access indefinitely (inside -3 the link is suspended for the stay, under LP-004.2), which is part of what makes elective residency a different civilizational category from permanent reassignment or filing.

The rule has specific implications for -3 visitation that the continuity parity doctrine in R27 addresses. Under the origin-layer rule alone, a Main Layer visitor entering -3 would retain their Main Layer revival architecture. Article XXV.VI federal law closes this exploit. Visitors must disclose their backup vessel status, and under LP-004.2 backup vessel coverage does not cross the -3 boundary: entry requires documented vessel-link suspension, the link stays severed for the duration of the visit, and it is restored on exit. Death inside -3 is final for visitor and resident alike. The origin-layer rule operates as the default, and the suspension operates as the targeted exception that keeps the default from producing visitor exploitation of -3’s mortality-priced economy.

The Refusal Option

The civilization commits to continuity without compelling it. Whitepaper §17.2 establishes that a citizen may refuse revival: the system preserves the option but does not impose it. Removing the implant and choosing not to continue is an exercise of the autonomy the architecture is specifically designed to preserve.

This has resident-level implications the bailout problem analyzed below does not fully capture. A resident in the upper layers facing the optimization paradox, terminal illness the augmentation infrastructure cannot resolve, or simply the felt exhaustion of a centuries-long life has the architectural option to refuse further revival. Refusal involves neither reassignment nor -3 descent. It is terminal non-continuation for a resident whose layer placement does not require it and whose conduct has not forfeited continuity, a death the civilization accepts because the citizen has chosen it. The choice is numerically rare, but its availability matters: the civilization’s commitment to continuity presumes the citizen wants continuity, and the refusal option preserves room for the cases where that presumption does not hold.

Refusal differs from -3 descent because it is an exercise of autonomy rather than a consequence of conduct. At the moment of the choice, a resident who refuses revival remains a resident of their current layer with their current record, and their death occurs under the architecture that applies to that position. The vessel’s subsequent disposition (decommissioning, archival, or eventual repurposing) falls into the custodianship gap analyzed later in this resource, because the civilization has not specified doctrine for vessel disposition following deliberate non-continuation. The gap is one of the architecture’s unresolved edges, and the first case on vessel custody after refused revival to reach the Supreme Court’s novelty filter will establish doctrine the current framework leaves open.

Terminal severance, in context. Whitepaper §17.3 frames -3 terminal severance as the civilization’s heaviest structural consequence. It removes continuity itself, which weighs more than incarceration or economic penalty. The civilization preserves life for everyone who has not crossed the terminal threshold and removes the guarantee for those who have. The framing matters for the architecture’s coherence because it ties the backup vessel system’s central commitment to a concrete function: it is the commitment that makes terminal severance meaningful as a consequence. A civilization without backup vessel architecture would have nothing to remove at the terminal threshold, and -3 would become a different kind of layer entirely. The continuity infrastructure the upper layers depend on and the terminal severance the -3 architecture depends on are one commitment seen from opposite ends: continuity is available, and removing it is what the civilization does when conduct crosses its sharpest line.

The Captured-Cognition Problem

Q22’s A+ response identifies the tension between the privacy architecture (cognition is non-public, no consequence) and the continuity architecture (full-fidelity mind-state capture, which necessarily includes unexpressed cognition). The A+ response treats this as a genuine constitutional novelty awaiting Supreme Court adjudication under Article XXI’s novelty filter, and infers that the Court would rule in favor of the privacy principle to keep the implant from becoming a thought-policing device through architectural side effect. The inference is defensible. The next step is to ask what the ruling has to do, and what it cannot accomplish however it rules.

The ruling must classify captured-but-unexpressed cognition as cognition for consequence purposes. A draft message composed and not sent remains cognition. A witnessed event the resident did not intervene in remains cognition about the event rather than outward action. Physiological stress responses, emotional states and reasoning patterns the resident did not consolidate into action are all cognition, and the privacy architecture’s commitment applies to them. The captured data exists, but it cannot produce consequences the doctrine does not attach to cognition generally.

What the ruling cannot do is keep the data inaccessible. The captured substrate resides in the backup vessel’s mind-state reservoir and in the fabrication proxy’s archives during the revival process. Access to this data is technologically possible for any actor with sufficient institutional standing, and the architecture preserves the data because revival requires it. A ruling that the data must not produce consequences does not make it disappear. It sets the norm but cannot make violating the norm architecturally impossible. A future administration, a future corrupted institution, or a future actor with access to the proxy installation’s archives could in principle retrieve captured cognition and use it for purposes the doctrine forbids. The ruling defines the violation without preventing it.

Stated plainly, the captured-cognition problem is this. The doctrine can forbid using captured cognition against residents, but it cannot prevent the capture, because the capture produces the continuity the civilization is committed to. Preventing captured cognition from existing would mean giving up sync, and revival would then have no mind-state to restore. The civilization cannot have both zero captured cognition and full-fidelity revival. In this specific respect the architecture holds continuity above privacy, and the Supreme Court’s ruling on the privacy principle compensates for that choice without resolving the underlying tension.

Q22’s A+ closes with an observation about Yara’s right to access her own captured cognition. If granted, that right becomes the resident-facing side of the same architectural tension. A resident who can retrieve their own captured cognition can also share it voluntarily, which makes the cognition recoverable for any purpose the resident consents to. Employers could require it as a condition of employment, though the Charter’s consent provisions would likely strike this down. Partners could request it as a trust signal, with unpredictable relational consequences. The civilization does not typically face the question of what residents will do when given access to data the doctrine classifies as private. The backup vessel architecture is where the question arrives, and the answer is not yet settled.

The Vessel Custodianship Question

A backup vessel is a physical object. It sits in a fabrication proxy installation somewhere, maintained by infrastructure the resident does not typically see and funded by economic flows the resident does not typically manage. Current doctrine is not well developed on who owns the vessel, who controls it, and what happens to it across the resident’s life transitions.

The simplest case is a resident in the upper layers with continuous implant coverage, whose vessel is maintained through institutional infrastructure. The vessel exists throughout the resident’s life, receives each sync, and is available for revival when needed. The resident does not own the vessel in any property-law sense; it is institutional infrastructure operating on the resident’s behalf. Nor does any specific institutional actor own it in a way that would allow the actor to dispose of it independently. The vessel belongs to the resident in function but not in property.

Complications arise at life transitions. When a resident descends to -1 or -2 through punitive reassignment, their vessel’s maintenance transitions from upper-layer infrastructure to the lower-layer fabrication proxy. Does the vessel physically relocate? Is a new vessel fabricated in the destination layer? Does the original persist in its original location while a new sync relationship forms with the lower-layer infrastructure? The doctrine does not specify, and the implementation likely varies with the specific transition’s conditions. For most practical purposes the resident does not experience the transition, since their continuity carries through, but the architectural details matter when transitions cascade or interact.

The sharper case is terminal reassignment to -3. The implant severs the backup vessel link at the hardware level. The resident is not going to use the vessel again, so its later state is not their concern. But the vessel itself continues to exist somewhere, because the fabrication proxy’s infrastructure does not immediately destroy every vessel whose owner’s link has been severed. What happens to a severed vessel is not well-documented. Options include immediate decommissioning (total material loss), archived preservation (cold storage, kept for some period), or repurposing (biological feedstock reclaimed for future fabrications). The civilization’s practice is likely some combination calibrated to the specific facility’s capacity and institutional framework. The resident has no say.

The still sharper case is a resident who dies without being revived. The mind-state reservoir contains their pre-death state, and the vessel exists in revival-ready condition. Whitepaper §17.2 lets the resident refuse revival, either through a refusal registered in advance or by declining during the revival window, and the refusal is honoured. What the doctrine does not settle is what happens next to the revival-ready vessel and its stored mind-state, or what the default is for a resident who left no instruction at all. The Charter has no mechanism for specifying vessel disposition in advance.

The custodianship gap is a genuine doctrinal hole. Most residents are not currently aware of it because their circumstances do not force the question. The first resident to contest the default, by contesting what happens to their vessel after a refused revival, will bring the question to the Supreme Court’s novelty filter, and the ruling will establish doctrine the current architecture has not specified.

The Bailout Problem

The layer -1 sociology acknowledges that a resident experiencing unbearable circumstances may choose to die deliberately, triggering backup vessel revival and resetting their immediate physical circumstances. The architecture supports this: the implant ledger records the deliberate termination, the fabrication proxy completes the revival, and the resident wakes up in a new vessel with their continuity preserved. The bailout works as an escape valve from circumstances the resident finds intolerable.

The doctrine does not clearly specify what stops iteration. A resident in -2 experiencing ongoing torment from local conditions has, in principle, the option to bail out. Nothing guarantees different circumstances after revival: they are still in -2, they still face whatever local situation produced the torment, and the bailout has reset only their immediate physical state. If the local situation persists, the resident may bail out again, and again. The architecture does not currently specify an iteration limit.

This produces several interrelated problems. First, high iteration counts strain the civilization’s commitment to continuity. A resident who has bailed out twelve times within a decade has accumulated twelve revival events, twelve vessel fabrications and twelve sync restorations, and repeat bailouts absorb the fabrication proxy facility’s capacity in ways the infrastructure was not designed for at scale. Second, the resident’s own relationship to continuity may degrade under high iteration. Each revival resets physical circumstance but preserves cognitive continuity, so the resident remembers bailing out previously and arrives with the accumulated experience of the pattern. At some point the continuity itself becomes the circumstance the resident wishes to escape, and bailout cannot resolve that, because bailout specifically preserves continuity.

Third, and most important for the architecture, bailout as a pattern produces gameable behavior that the consequence architecture does not account for. A resident in -2 facing a specific bad actor who cannot legally be stopped (the layer permits non-lethal harm) can use bailout to escape immediate torment, but the bad actor’s presence persists. Repeated bailout becomes the resident’s adaptation to a layer feature the consequence architecture expected residents to accept as their condition. The architecture did not leave non-lethal harm in -2 unprevented in order to produce a population using revival as a workaround. It left that harm unprevented to express the layer’s withdrawal from protection against it. Bailout at scale degrades the expression.

The civilization’s current practice on bailout is permissive, with no iteration limits, no consequences and no institutional friction. It reflects the assumption that residents choosing deliberate termination are exercising legitimate autonomy and should not be obstructed. The assumption holds at low iteration counts and begins to break down at high ones, and the architecture does not yet have a mechanism to distinguish a resident’s one-time escape from intolerable acute conditions from systematic use of bailout as a chronic management tool. The bailout problem is one of the architecture’s unresolved edges, and the civilization will eventually need to address it at the level of doctrine rather than practice.

The Student’s Error

The student who reads this resource and concludes that backup vessel architecture solves mortality has misread the architecture. It relocates mortality from the continuous background condition of biological existence to a constrained set of failure modes whose combined rate is much lower than the biological baseline but is not zero. The civilization’s residents still die. They die much less often than Earth-era residents did, and when they die the probability of revival is high enough that most residents do not experience death as terminal. But the 10–50x effective multiplier is not a small number, and across centuries of lifespan the cumulative exposure produces a meaningful population of residents for whom the architecture failed. The revival failure rate published in the layer documentation is the biological floor, and the rate residents actually face is higher.

Full-fidelity sync does not resolve the identity question, though it is often taken to. The question remains philosophically open. The architecture renders it operationally inert: the civilization treats the revived resident as the same resident regardless of what view the resident or anyone else takes of the underlying metaphysics. That is a doctrinal choice rather than a philosophical answer. A civilization that made the opposite doctrinal choice, treating revival as producing a new resident requiring fresh classification, would produce different institutional effects without altering the metaphysical question at all. The architecture relies on keeping the operational position and the metaphysical position separate.

Nor is the Q22 gap a malfunction. Sync is periodic, so a revived resident wakes at their last sync, and Yara’s 47 minutes are what the architecture ordinarily produces. The gap raises genuine philosophical questions (captured-cognition, consent to recovery, what identity consists of), and the design puts them there.

Finally, the rest of the civilization’s doctrine depends on backup vessel architecture without adequately addressing it. Pre-intervention enforcement assumes revival is available for the rare cases where it fails. Post-intervention enforcement assumes revival is available to restore victims. Layer reassignment as consequence assumes the resident continues to exist to experience the reassignment. Voluntary permanent residency assumes continuity through the descent. Even the -3 Terminal hardware-severance design depends on backup vessel architecture existing in the layers above it, because the severance is meaningful only as the removal of a commitment the other layers honour. Every other architectural commitment the civilization makes rests on this one, and the captured-cognition problem, the custodianship gap, and the bailout question are its unresolved edges. Future doctrine will have to address them. When it does, the answers will likely reshape the surrounding architecture as well. The continuity infrastructure is a project the civilization is still building.

Resource 29: The Monetary Silo

An Economics Textbook Excerpt — Cross-Layer Transfer, the Retirement Mechanic, and the Circulation Question

VMSS operates four siloed currency economies stacked along the layer gradient. +1 Sanctuary and Main Layer share a common currency; -1, -2, and -3 each maintain separate currencies that do not convert upward under any circumstance. Downward conversion is permitted only through authorized channels and only under a retention schedule that retires origin-layer currency at the conversion point. The architecture raises design questions the civilization’s economists return to periodically: why retire currency at the conversion boundary rather than recirculate it, what the retirement mechanic produces across the aggregate economy, and whether the design reflects necessary architectural commitments or a legacy preference the civilization could reopen without damage. This resource examines the monetary silo architecture from the ground up. It treats the retirement-versus-recirculation question as open, because both sides have real arguments and the civilization’s choice is defensible without being uniquely correct.

The Architectural Problem Siloing Solves

A currency that circulated freely across every layer of the civilization would open an arbitrage channel the layer system cannot survive. A resident who earned purchasing power in Main Layer conditions (robust institutional supply, market-priced goods, extensive fabrication infrastructure) could carry it into -3 Terminal, where the same currency commands two-and-a-half to four times the real consumption. The wealth gradient would invert the punitive gradient. Lower-layer residence, which the architecture positions as a restriction of institutional benefit, would become economically advantageous for any resident with accumulated upper-layer capital, and the layer system’s moral geometry would collapse under that pressure.

The same failure runs in the opposite direction. If lower-layer residents could convert their currency upward at face value, the -3 economy, with its Earth-analogous capitalism, equity markets, private banking and absence of VMSS circulation oversight, would become a staging ground for moving capital into Sanctuary and Main without passing through the civilization’s anti-concentration instruments. The Savings Circulation Mandate, the tiered progressive taxation, and the property ownership caps all operate on the upper-layer currency. An unrestricted upward channel from a layer that does not honor those constraints would let capital accumulate at the rate the constraint-free economy allows and then enter the constrained economy at face value, bypassing every anti-oligarchy instrument the architecture runs.

Currency siloing closes both failure modes at once. Each layer’s economy is self-contained, and purchasing power accumulated within a layer cannot be exported at face value to a layer where it would mean something different. Residents are not prohibited from moving; their value simply does not move with them except through the channels the architecture specifies. That is how the silo protects the layer system’s moral and institutional geometry at the economic level.

The Mechanics of the Silo

The architecture runs on four distinct currency units. +1 Sanctuary and Main Layer share a single currency; the two upper layers are functionally one monetary zone because their populations move freely between them and the institutional infrastructure is continuous. -1 Noncompliance, -2 Violent Offense, and -3 Terminal each maintain separate currencies that exist only within their own layer economies. The central bank, the only genuinely new institution VMSS introduces with no direct Earth analog, is sole issuing authority for all four currency units and operates as infrastructure rather than policy. It does not set interest rates or engage in monetary stimulus. It maintains the integrity of the silo architecture and executes settlement when currency crosses layer boundaries.

Upward conversion is prohibited without exception. A -3 resident cannot exchange freedom tokens for Main Layer currency under any circumstance: not at the central bank directly, not through intermediated channels, and not through goods arbitrage, because cross-layer goods trade settles in the destination layer’s currency at rates adjusted for the purchasing power gradient (PPG) rather than through currency exchange. A resident who returns upward from elective residency carries their citizenship upward but not their accumulated lower-layer currency. That currency stays in the economy where it was earned, and the resident arrives in the upper layer economically neutral and must earn upper-layer currency through upper-layer work.

External inconvertibility runs in parallel. VMSS currency does not circulate outside the civilization’s borders, and foreign nationals cannot acquire it by exchange. International trade runs on a goods-for-goods or goods-for-foreign-currency basis, with the central bank as the counterparty: it absorbs foreign currency and releases VMSS currency only into the internal layer economies. An ally nation trading with VMSS delivers goods or foreign currency, receives goods or service access, and never holds VMSS layer currency as a tradeable instrument. The silo is hermetic, externally as well as internally.

Downward Transfer Channels and the Gradient

Three categories of resident can move value downward across the silo boundary. Visitors descending temporarily may convert a portion of their upper-layer assets under the downward transfer retention schedule (1 to 10 percent of net worth, depending on the visitor’s total assets) into destination-layer currency at the applicable purchasing power gradient multiplier. Elective residents, who keep upper-layer citizenship while living in a lower layer, follow the same retention schedule and conversion mechanics. Voluntary permanent residents, who sever upper-layer citizenship entirely to descend, fall under the progressive liquidation schedule, set in federal law under LP-076 with Article III.V holding the principle. They retain 1 to 10 percent of liquidated assets, converted to destination-layer currency. The remainder, 90 to 99 percent depending on the filing wealth bracket, is absorbed into the Automation Dividend Treasury as the civilization’s institutional absorption of the departing resident’s upper-layer capital.

The retention schedule is progressive because the civilization treats large-capital descent as a stronger signal than small-capital descent. A resident descending with modest net worth retains a higher proportion: the absolute value is unlikely to distort the destination economy, and the retention matters proportionally more to the resident’s post-descent life. A resident descending with substantial net worth retains a lower proportion, because even the reduced proportion is large in destination-layer terms and because the architecture treats large-capital descent as potentially strategic, warranting stronger institutional absorption.

The purchasing power gradient at the conversion point is a range that the central bank derives from observed economic conditions within the destination layer, rather than a fixed exchange rate. -1 converts at approximately 1.3 to 1.8 times Main purchasing power, -2 at approximately 1.8 to 2.5 times, and -3 at approximately 2.5 to 4 times. The range accommodates scarcity conditions, institutional withdrawal, and tourism-inflow pressure, which vary across a layer’s districts and produce real economic variance within a single layer. A tourist-heavy -3 district and a remote cooperative district operate under different PPG conditions at the same time, and the central bank’s settlement rate reflects the conditions of the transferring resident’s destination district rather than a uniform layer-wide average.

The retention schedule and the PPG multiplier compound. A Main Layer resident with ten million units who elects voluntary permanent residency in -3 retains, at the middle of the retention schedule, approximately five percent: five hundred thousand units. Those units convert at the -3 PPG into somewhere between 125,000 and 200,000 -3 freedom tokens, depending on settlement conditions. Fewer units come out than went in because each freedom token buys two and a half to four times what a Main unit buys; the conversion preserves real purchasing power rather than multiplying it. In -3 purchasing power terms the resident arrives with substantial local wealth; in upper-layer terms they leave with a small fraction of what they held. The combination is intentional. The descent is not economically trivial at the destination, yet the upper-layer economy loses almost nothing from the departure, because ADT absorption keeps almost all of the resident’s upper-layer capital in the upper-layer economy.

Retirement and Recirculation — Where Each Operates

The architecture already runs two distinct value-handling mechanisms at the silo boundary, and the distinction is central to the design question this resource addresses.

Recirculation operates on the portion of descended capital that stays in the upper-layer economy. The 90-to-99 percent liquidation under voluntary permanent residency, the full 100 percent liquidation under involuntary descent, the SCM garnishing that activates when district aggregate savings cross the threshold, and the tiered progressive taxation on upper-layer income all flow into the Automation Dividend Treasury. The ADT redistributes these flows as UBI: $10,000 per month per resident in the upper layers, $5,000 in -1, $2,500 in -2, $1,250 in -3, untaxed, unconditional, and paid to every citizen including newborns. The loop is tight and closed, returning capital absorbed by institutional mechanisms to the population as the dividend of the automated economy. Upper-layer recirculation stays in upper-layer currency and lower-layer recirculation stays in lower-layer currency, so recirculation never moves currency across silos.

Retirement operates at the conversion point itself. When a resident crosses the silo boundary with their retained 1-to-10 percent, the central bank retires origin-layer currency equal to the converted value and issues destination-layer currency equal to the PPG-adjusted converted value. The origin-layer currency is extinguished rather than transferred anywhere; no currency unit crosses the silo, because that would violate currency siloing. The destination-layer currency is freshly issued at the applicable PPG rate. The resident arrives in the destination economy with newly issued local currency, and the origin economy loses the corresponding value from circulation at the moment of conversion.

The two mechanisms serve different architectural functions. Recirculation returns capital to the UBI stream, so that the civilization’s institutional absorption becomes every citizen’s dividend. Retirement preserves the silo invariant, so that currency from one economy cannot appear as currency in another. Both operate under current doctrine, and each is necessary where it operates. Since the architecture already uses both correctly, the design question is narrower than a choice between them: it concerns the specific slice of value that moves across the silo at the conversion point.

The Narrow Question

When a resident converts their retained 1-to-10 percent at the silo boundary, the origin-layer currency is retired and the destination-layer currency is newly issued. The value that existed in the origin economy is gone from it, and the value that appears in the destination economy is net new issuance rather than a transfer of existing currency.

A designer examining this mechanism can reasonably ask why the origin-layer currency has to be retired. The central bank is already sole issuing authority for both currencies, and the destination-layer currency is freshly issued either way, because currency units cannot cross. Retiring the origin-layer currency is an architectural choice rather than an architectural necessity. The central bank could instead absorb the origin-layer currency into the Automation Dividend Treasury and redistribute it as upper-layer UBI, while issuing the destination-layer currency at the PPG rate as before. The silo invariant would hold, since no origin-layer currency crosses, and the destination economy would receive the same net issuance it receives under the current mechanism. The only difference is what happens to the origin-layer value: it disappears (retirement), or it flows through ADT to the upper-layer population as UBI (recirculation).

The question points to a design decision rather than a doctrinal flaw or architectural error. Current doctrine has made the decision and could revisit it without violating the silo invariant, changing the retention schedule, altering the PPG mechanics, or disrupting any other element of the monetary architecture. The decision is internal to the conversion-point mechanism, and the two designs produce outputs similar enough that reasonable economists can disagree about which is preferable.

The Case for Retirement

Retirement has a mild deflationary effect on the origin-layer economy. Every cross-silo conversion removes currency from circulation without a corresponding reduction in goods, services, or productive capacity. Over time, if cross-silo conversions outpace central bank issuance into the upper-layer economy, the upper-layer money supply contracts relative to the real economy and purchasing power per upper-layer unit rises. Residents holding upper-layer currency see a small, gradual appreciation in what it commands.

The effect fits the civilization’s anti-concentration posture. A gently deflationary monetary environment rewards savers modestly, strengthens the upper-layer currency’s relationship to the goods it purchases, and slightly compresses the gradient between upper-layer and lower-layer purchasing power, because a relatively stronger upper-layer currency narrows the PPG advantage lower-layer residents experience. A civilization that values stable monetary conditions, and wants mild deflationary pressure to counterbalance the inflationary pressure of the ADT’s continuous UBI issuance, can defensibly argue that retirement is the correct mechanism at the conversion boundary.

Retirement is also definitionally clean. Currency leaves one silo and currency enters the other, and the two are never the same unit. The central bank acts as pure infrastructure at the conversion point, performing issuance and retirement symmetrically. No ADT routing is required, and cross-silo conversion volume affects no monthly UBI distribution, so the monetary plumbing stays decoupled from the fiscal plumbing. A central bank that retires at conversion has a narrower operational remit than one that also routes origin-layer conversions through the fiscal stream, and VMSS’s design philosophy values institutional narrowness.

The Case for Recirculation

Recirculation at the conversion point would redirect the origin-layer value into the Automation Dividend Treasury, from which it would flow to the upper-layer population as UBI. The currency still does not cross, so the silo invariant holds. Instead of being extinguished, though, the origin-layer value stays in the origin-layer economy, redistributed to every upper-layer citizen as a fractional increment to their monthly UBI.

The aggregate effect is small, since cross-silo conversion volume is a small fraction of total upper-layer economic activity, but it is positive-sum for the upper-layer population in a way retirement is not. Retirement benefits upper-layer currency holders diffusely through mild deflation. Recirculation benefits the entire upper-layer citizenry directly through a fractional UBI uplift that compounds across every cross-silo conversion. Each descending resident’s conversion would add a tiny UBI contribution to the population they are leaving, instead of removing that value from circulation.

The coherence argument runs the other way here. Recirculation is already the dominant institutional absorption mechanism: the 90-to-99 percent liquidation, the involuntary-descent 100 percent liquidation, the SCM garnishing, and the tiered taxation all flow to the ADT. Under the current design, the retained 1-to-10 percent slice is the only category of descending capital that bypasses ADT. The architecture does not reject recirculation for this slice; the silo mechanics simply happen to retire the currency at the conversion point. Recirculation at conversion would make the institutions consistent, with every channel through which capital leaves the upper-layer economy flowing to the ADT without exception, which is what the Treasury is architecturally for.

The deflationary counter-argument weakens under examination. The upper-layer economy does not lack deflationary pressure. The SCM, the tiered taxation, and the property ownership caps already operate as anti-inflationary instruments at scale, and they, not currency retirement at the margins, offset the ADT’s continuous UBI issuance.
